A Deep Inference System for Differential Linear Logic
Matteo Acclavio, Giulio Guerrieri

Abstract
Differential linear logic ( 𝖣𝗂𝖫𝖫 \mathsf{DiLL} ) provides a fine analysis of resource consumption in cut-elimination. We investigate the subsystem of 𝖣𝗂𝖫𝖫 \mathsf{DiLL} without promotion in a deep inference formalism, where cuts are at an atomic level. In our system every provable formula admits a derivation in normal form, via a normalization procedure that commutes with the translation from sequent calculus to deep inference.



1
Introduction

Girard
[
14
]
introduced linear logic (
\mathsf{LL}
) as a refinement of intuitionistic and classical logics, built around cut-elimination.
In
\mathsf{LL}
, a pair of dual modalities (the
exponentials

\oc
and
\wn
) give a logical status to the operations of erasing and copying (sub-)proofs in the cut-elimination procedure.
The idea is that
linear
proofs (
i.e.
proofs without exponentials) use their hypotheses exactly once,
whilst
exponential
proofs may use their hypotheses at will.
In particular, the
promotion rule
makes a (sub-)proof available to be erased or copied an unbounded number of times, provided that its hypotheses are as well (it is a contextual rule).
Via Curry–Howard correspondence between programs and proofs,
\mathsf{LL}
gives a logical status to the operations of erasing and copying data in the evaluation process.
Linear proofs correspond to programs which call their arguments exactly once, exponential proofs to programs which call their arguments at will.
The study of
\mathsf{LL}
contributed to unveil the logical nature of resource consumption.

The importance of being differential.

A further tool for the analysis of resource consumption in cut-elimination
came from Ehrhard and Regnier’s work on
differential
\lambda
-calculus
[
8
]
and
differential linear logic
(
\mathsf{DiLL}
,
[
10
,
29
]
).
Despite the fact that
\mathsf{DiLL}
is inconsistent (every sequent
\vdash\Gamma
can be proved), it has a cut-elimination theorem
[
29
,
13
]
and internalizes notions from denotational models of
\mathsf{LL}
into the syntax.
In particular,

\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
(the
promotion-free
fragment of
\mathsf{DiLL}
,
[
10
]
) is a logic corresponding to the semantic constructions defined by Ehrhard’s finiteness spaces
[
5
]
.
Finiteness spaces interpret linear proofs as linear functions on certain topological vector spaces, on which one can define an operation of derivative.
Exponential proofs are interpreted as analytic functions, in the sense that they can be arbitrarily approximated by the semantic equivalent of a Taylor expansion
[
5
,
6
]
, which becomes available thanks to the presence of a derivative operator.
In syntactic terms,
these constructions take an interesting form:
they correspond to “symmetrizing” the exponential modalities,
i.e.
in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}

the rules handling the dual exponential modalities
\oc
and
\wn
are perfectly symmetrical, although the logic is not self-dual.
Indeed, in
\mathsf{LL}
, only the promotion rule introduces the
\oc
modality, creating inputs that can be called an unbounded number of times.
In
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
the rules handling the
\oc
modality (
\oc
-dereliction
\oc\mathsf{d}
,
\oc
-contraction
\oc\mathsf{c}
,
\oc
-weakening
\oc\mathsf{w}
) are the duals of the usual rules dealing with the
\wn
modality (
\wn
-dereliction
\wn\mathsf{d}
,
\wn
-contraction
\wn\mathsf{c}
,
\wn
-weakening
\wn\mathsf{w}
).
In particular,
\oc
-dereliction
expresses in the syntax the semantic derivative: it releases inputs of type
\oc A
that must be called exactly
once
, so that executing a program
f
on a “
\oc
-derelicted” input
x
(
i.e.
performing cut-elimination on a proof
f
cut with a “
\oc
-derelicted” sub-proof
x
) amounts to compute the best linear approximation of
f
on
x
.
This imposes non-deterministic choices: if in an evaluation the program
f
needs several copies of the input
x
(
i.e.
if the proof
f
uses several times the hypothesis
\oc A
), then there are different executions of
f
on
x
, depending on which sub-routine (
i.e.
hypothesis) of
f
is fed with the unique available copy of
x
.
Thus we get a formal
sum
, where each term represents a possibility.
The rules
\oc
-contraction and
\oc
-weakening put together a finite (possibly
0
) number of copies of an input, so that it can be called a
bounded
number of times during execution.

What is also interesting is that
\mathsf{LL}
promotion rule can be encoded in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
through the notion of syntactic
Taylor expansion

[
9
,
11
,
27
,
30
,
16
,
4
,
17
,
18
]
:
a proof in
\mathsf{LL}
can be decomposed into a possibly infinite set of (promotion-free) proofs in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
.
Given a proof in
\mathsf{LL}
with exactly one promotion rule
\oc\mathsf{p}
, the idea is to replace
\oc\mathsf{p}
(which makes the resource
\pi
available at will)
with an infinite set of “differential” proofs in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
, each of them taking
n\in\mathbb{N}
copies of
\pi
so as to make the resource
\pi
available exactly
n
times.
The potential infinity of the promotion rule becomes an actual infinite via the Taylor expansion.

Nets vs. sequents.

The system
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
is usually presented in two formalisms: sequent calculus and Lafont’s interaction nets
[
25
]
(a graphical representation of proofs similar to
\mathsf{LL}
proof-nets).
The
symmetry
of the rules handling the dual exponentials

\oc
and
\wn
in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
is evident in interaction nets,
but not at all in the sequent calculus. In interaction nets for
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
, the rules for
\wn
and
\oc
have the
same geometry
:

So, the distinction between
\oc
and
\wn
is given only by their different behaviors in correctness graphs (a geometrical characterization of the interaction nets corresponding to proofs in the
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
sequent calculus).
But meaningful operations in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
such as cut-elimination can be defined directly on interaction nets, regardless of being correct or not.
The benefit is that
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
cut-elimination steps defined on interaction nets are perfectly symmetric: for instance, the step for a cut
\wn\mathsf{c}
/
\oc\mathsf{d}
is exactly the
dual
of the step for a cut
\oc\mathsf{c}
/
\wn\mathsf{d}
, and similarly for the other steps (see
[
29
, Fig. 4]
).

This elegant symmetry in the presentation of cut-elimination steps is lost in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
sequent calculus, see our
Figure

3
.
Moreover, cut-elimination in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
sequent calculus has to deal with (many) uninteresting commutative steps, while interaction nets get rid of them.
Thus, interaction nets allow one to express
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
cut-elimination with a sharper account than in sequent calculus.
Not by chance, all papers dealing with
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
cut-elimination use only interaction nets
[
10
,
29
,
35
,
13
,
36
,
31
]
.

However, the interaction net presentation of
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
has some flaws that do not affect the sequent calculus:
interaction nets do not have an
inductive tree-like structure
and so it is not easy to handle them.
Moreover, not all interaction nets correspond to a derivation in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
sequent calculus, a
global
geometrical correctness criterion is required to identify them.

Our contribution.

We define a proof system for
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
in the formalism of
open deduction

[
21
]
following the principles of
deep inference

[
22
,
3
,
20
,
32
,
39
]
.
Such a formalism, which allows rules to be applied deep in a context, provides a more flexible composition of derivations and makes explicit the behavior of the cut-elimination process in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
in a more fine-grained way, since it pushes cut-elimination at an
atomic
level.
Besides, our deep inference system for
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
gathers good qualities of both sequent calculus and interaction nets formalisms: it restores the interaction net
symmetries
lost in the sequent calculus and its derivations keep a handy
inductive
tree-like (or better, sequence-like) structure as in the sequent calculus, without the need for a global correctness criterion like in interaction nets.

A first attempt in the direction of a deep inference system for
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
is in
[
12
]
where, however, the
\mathsf{sum}
-rule is absent and, as a consequence, it is not suitable to represent the dynamic behavior of
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
.

To fully recover the expressiveness of this logic, we design our system to include a binary connective
+
which represents the sum operation.
The rules for
+
(and for its unit
\mathsf{0}
)
prevent the use of Guglielmi and Tubella’s general result
[
37
,
38
]
to show cut-elimination.
However, we are able to define a normalization procedure by rule permutations which fully captures the dynamics of
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
cut-elimination, in a way similar to the one in

[
33
,
34
]
.
Our system is sound and complete with respect to
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
sequent calculus, through a translation that commutes with cut-elimination/normalization.

In the normalization procedure, we can classify our rule permutations depending on their behavior: some rule permutations correspond to
multiplicative cut-elimination steps,
other permutations correspond to “resource management” cut-elimination steps
(involving the
\wn
and
\oc
rules),
other permutations correspond to “slice management” operations
(involving the propagation of
+
and
\mathsf{0}
).

2
Differential Linear Logic

We present here the classical, propositional, one-sided sequent calculus for
differential linear logic
without promotion (
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
).
The formulas of
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
are exactly the same as in the multiplicative exponential fragment of linear logic (
\mathsf{MELL}
).

\mathsf{MELL}
formulas
are defined by the grammar below, where
a,b,c,\dots
range over a countably infinite set of propositional variables:

A,B\Coloneqq a\mid\overline{a}\mid{\mathsf{1}}\mid\mathsf{\bot}\mid A\otimes B\mid A\invamp B\mid\oc A\mid\wn A

Linear negation
\overline{(\cdot)}
is defined through De Morgan laws so as to be involutive
(
\overline{\overline{A}}=A
for any
A
):

\overline{(a)}=\overline{a}\quad\overline{(\overline{a})}=a\quad\overline{A\otimes B}=\overline{A}\invamp\overline{B}\quad\overline{A\invamp B}=\overline{A}\otimes\overline{B}\quad\overline{{\mathsf{1}}}=\mathsf{\bot}\quad\overline{\mathsf{\bot}}={\mathsf{1}}\quad\overline{\oc A}=\wn\overline{A}\quad\overline{\wn A}=\oc\overline{A}

Variables and their negations are
atomic
;
\otimes,\invamp
are
multiplicative connectives
and
{\mathsf{1}},\mathsf{\bot}
are their respective
units
;
\oc,\wn
are
exponential modalities
.
A
\mathsf{MELL}
sequent
is a (finite) multiset of
\mathsf{MELL}
formulas
A_{1},\dots,A_{n}
(for any
n\geq 0
), and it is ranged over by
\Gamma,\Delta,\Sigma
.

\scriptstyle\;\mathsf{ax}

\vdash A,\overline{A}

\vdash\Gamma,A,B

\scriptstyle\;\invamp

\vdash\Gamma,A\invamp B

\vdash\Gamma,A

\vdash B,\Delta

\scriptstyle\;\otimes

\vdash\Gamma,A\otimes B,\Delta

\scriptstyle\;{\mathsf{1}}

\vdash{\mathsf{1}}

\vdash\Gamma

\scriptstyle\;\mathsf{\bot}

\vdash\Gamma,\mathsf{\bot}

\vdash\Gamma,A

\vdash\overline{A},\Delta

\scriptstyle\;\mathsf{cut}

\vdash\Gamma,\Delta

\vdash\Gamma,A

\scriptstyle\;\oc\mathsf{d}

\vdash\Gamma,\oc A

\vdash\Gamma,A

\scriptstyle\;\wn\mathsf{d}

\vdash\Gamma,\wn A

\vdash\Gamma,\oc A

\vdash\oc A,\Delta

\scriptstyle\;\oc\mathsf{c}

\vdash\Gamma,\oc A,\Delta

\vdash\Gamma,\wn A,\wn A

\scriptstyle\;\wn\mathsf{c}

\vdash\Gamma,\wn A

\scriptstyle\;\oc\mathsf{w}

\vdash\oc A

\vdash\Gamma

\scriptstyle\;\wn\mathsf{w}

\vdash\Gamma,\wn A

\vdash\Gamma

\vdash\Gamma

\scriptstyle\;\mathsf{sum}

\vdash\Gamma

\scriptstyle\;\mathsf{zero}

\vdash\Gamma

\vdash\wn\Gamma,A

\scriptstyle\;\mathsf{\oc p}

\vdash\wn\Gamma,\oc A

Figure 1:
Sequent calculus rules for
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
(on the left) and the promotion rule (on the right)
[
29
]
.

Figure

1
gives the sequent calculus rules

1

1

Usually, in the literature on
\mathsf{LL}
and
\mathsf{DiLL}
, the rules
\wn\mathsf{w}
,
\wn\mathsf{d}
,
\wn\mathsf{c}
,
\oc\mathsf{w}
,
\oc\mathsf{d}
,
\oc\mathsf{c}
are called respectively weakening, dereliction, contraction, co-weakening, co-dereliction and co-contraction.
To avoid clashes with the usual terminology in deep inference (see
Footnote

4
), we call them
\wn
-weakening,
\wn
-dereliction,
\wn
-contraction,
\oc
-weakening,
\oc
-dereliction and
\oc
-contraction, respectively.

for
differential linear logic

\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
(without
promotion

\mathsf{\oc p}
);
the rules on the first line correspond to the
multiplicative linear logic
fragment
\mathsf{MLL}
.
We set:

\begin{array}[]{l@{\hskip 2em}l@{\hskip 2em}l}\mathsf{MELL}=\mathsf{MLL}\cup\{\wn\mathsf{w},\wn\mathsf{d},\wn\mathsf{c},\mathsf{\oc p}\}\hfil\qquad&\hfil\qquad&\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}^{-}=\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}\setminus\{\mathsf{zero},\mathsf{sum}\}\end{array}

We define
\equiv
as the least congruence on
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
derivations generated by the relations in
Figure

2
.
Roughly, the rule
\mathsf{zero}
plays the role of annihilating element with respect to all the other rules but
\mathsf{sum}
, for which it is a neutral element;
whilst the rule
\mathsf{sum}
commutes with any rule below it.
Clearly,
\equiv
preserves conclusions and can be oriented so as to define a terminating rewriting relation that pushes down the rules
\mathsf{zero}
and
\mathsf{sum}
in a derivation.
As a consequence, every derivation in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
can be rewritten in a
canonical form
(with the same conclusion).

Definition 2.1

(Canonical form, slice)
.

Let
\pi
be a derivation in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
:

1.

\pi
is a
slice
if it is in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}^{-}
(
i.e.
the rules
\mathsf{zero}
and
\mathsf{sum}
do not occur in
\pi
);

2.

\pi
is
canonical
or in
canonical form
if
either it consists of a
\mathsf{zero}
rule, or it is a slice, or if its last rule is
\mathsf{sum}

with a canonical form as left premise and a slice as right premise.

A
canonical form
of
\pi
is any canonical derivation
\pi^{\prime}
in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
such that
\pi\equiv\pi^{\prime}
.

Fact 2.2

(Canonicity)
.

Any derivation in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
has a canonical form (with the same conclusion).

Intuitively, considering only canonical derivations, slices—
i.e.
derivations in the subsystem
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}^{-}
—are the “real and meaningful” proofs in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
(corresponding to simple nets in
[
10
,
27
,
29
,
35
]
),
while the rules
\mathsf{sum}
and
\mathsf{zero}
are needed to define cut-elimination in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
, in particular they ensure that the conclusion of a derivation is preserved after a cut-elimination step (the subsystem
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}^{-}
is not closed under cut-elimination, see below).
The rule
\mathsf{sum}
puts together slices with the same conclusions
\vdash\!\Gamma
, similarly to multiset union: it expresses the possibility of several “real proofs” of
\vdash\!\Gamma
.
The rule
\mathsf{zero}
then corresponds to the empty multiset of “real proofs” of
\vdash\!\Gamma
: it claims
\vdash\!\Gamma
without a proof (it is reminiscent of daimon in ludics
[
15
]
).
Because of it, any
\mathsf{MELL}
sequent (also the empty one) is provable in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
.

Figure 2:
The congruence
\equiv
on derivations generated by the rules
\mathsf{zero}
and
\mathsf{sum}
in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
, where
{\mathsf{r}_{1}}
is any unary rule in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
, and
{\mathsf{r}_{2}}
is any binary rule in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
but
\mathsf{sum}
.

Let
\pi
be a derivation in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
.
We say that
\pi
is
with atomic axioms
(or
\eta
-expanded
) if every instance of the rule
\mathsf{ax}
introduces a
\mathsf{MELL}
sequent of the form
\vdash a,\overline{a}
, where
a
is a propositional variable.
We say that
\pi
is
cut-free
is it does not contain any instance of the rule
\mathsf{cut}
,
i.e.

\pi
is a derivation in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}\setminus\{\mathsf{cut}\}
.

Proposition 2.3

(Atomic axioms)
.

For every derivation
\pi
in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
with conclusion
\vdash\Gamma
, there exists a
\eta
-expanded derivation
\pi^{\prime}
in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
with conclusion
\vdash\Gamma
.
If, moreover,
\pi
is canonical (resp. a slice) then
\pi^{\prime}
is canonical (resp. a slice).

Proof.

Rewrite any non-atomic instance of the rule
\mathsf{ax}
according to the
\eta
-expansion
relation
\rightsquigarrow_{\eta}
below:

\footnotesize{\box_set_eq_clear:cN{g_{_{e}}bproof_{b}ox_{1}}\char 15\relax\hbox{$\vbox{}$}}\rightsquigarrow_{\eta}{\box_set_eq_clear:cN{g_{_{e}}bproof_{b}ox_{1}}\char 15\relax\hbox{$\vbox{}$}}\hskip 17.00024pt\hskip 17.00024pt{\box_set_eq_clear:cN{g_{_{e}}bproof_{b}ox_{1}}\char 15\relax\hbox{$\vbox{}$}}\rightsquigarrow_{\eta}~{\box_set_eq_clear:cN{g_{_{e}}bproof_{b}ox_{1}}\char 15\relax\hbox{$\vbox{}$}}\hskip 17.00024pt\hskip 17.00024pt{\box_set_eq_clear:cN{g_{_{e}}bproof_{b}ox_{1}}\char 15\relax\hbox{$\vbox{}$}}\rightsquigarrow_{\eta}~{\box_set_eq_clear:cN{g_{_{e}}bproof_{b}ox_{1}}\char 15\relax\hbox{$\vbox{}$}}

(1)

It is immediate to prove that the relation
\rightsquigarrow_{\eta}
on the derivations of
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
is terminating.
∎

Figure 3:
Key cases of cut-elimination rewriting rules for
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
sequent calculus (colors highlight
\mathsf{cut}
-relations between formula occurrences).

Cut-elimination.

Despite its incoherence,
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
provides a fine analysis of resource consumption in cut-elimination.
Rewriting rules
\rightsquigarrow_{\mathsf{cut}}
for cut-elimination in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
sequent calculus are defined in
Figure

3
.
They are just the formulation in the sequent calculus formalism of the cut-elimination steps defined and studied in
[
10
,
35
,
13
]
and
[
29
, Fig. 4]
within the interaction nets formalism.
We represent in
Figure

3
only the
key cases
, where the principal connectives in the cut formulas are dual (the pairs of dual connectives are
\otimes/\invamp
,
{\mathsf{1}}/\mathsf{\bot}
,
\oc/\wn
).
The way
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
deals with the
commutative cases
is omitted since is analogous to usual sequent calculi.
With these cut-elimination steps it has been proved in
[
10
,
29
,
13
]
that the rule
\mathsf{cut}
is admissible in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
(and even in
\mathsf{DiLL}
,
i.e.
, the system
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
plus
\mathsf{MELL}
promotion
\mathsf{\oc p}
).

Theorem 2.4

(Cut-elimination,
[
10
,
29
,
13
]
)
.

For every derivation
\pi
in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
with conclusion
\vdash\Gamma
, there exists a cut-free derivation
\pi^{\prime}
in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
with conclusion
\vdash\Gamma
such that
\pi\rightsquigarrow_{\mathsf{cut}}^{*}\pi^{\prime}
.

Cut-elimination preserves atomic axioms: if
\pi\rightsquigarrow_{\mathsf{cut}}\pi^{\prime}
and
\pi
is
\eta
-expanded, then
\pi^{\prime}
is
\eta
-expanded.
Note that if
\pi\rightsquigarrow_{\mathsf{cut}}\pi^{\prime}
with
\pi
canonical then
\pi^{\prime}
is not necessarily canonical (
e.g.
if in
\pi
a cut
\wn\mathsf{c}/\oc\mathsf{d}
or
\wn\mathsf{d}/\oc\mathsf{w}
is above another rule), but
\pi^{\prime}
can be rewritten in a canonical form (see
2.2
above).
Indeed,
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}^{-}
is not closed under cut-elimination: steps
\wn\mathsf{c}/\oc\mathsf{d}
or
\wn\mathsf{d}/\oc\mathsf{w}
create instances of the rule
\mathsf{sum}
or
\mathsf{zero}
.

To explain the importance of the rules
\mathsf{sum}
and
\mathsf{zero}
as
resource management
, we give an informal account of the cut-elimination steps in
Figure

3
for the key cases involving
\oc/\wn
.
Roughly, they follow the “law of supply and demand” so as to be
resource-sensitive
: in each slice no duplication or erasure is allowed.
The rules for
\wn
(
\wn\mathsf{w}
,
\wn\mathsf{d}
,
\wn\mathsf{c}
)
ask for
a number of resources of type
\oc A
(
0
,
1
, and the sum of the numbers asked by its premises, respectively), while the rules for
\oc
(
\oc\mathsf{w}
,
\oc\mathsf{d}
,
\oc\mathsf{c}
)
supply
a number of resources of type
\oc A
(
0
,
1
, and the sum of the numbers supplied by its premises, respectively). Cases:

1.

If the numbers of demanded and supplied resources match, the cut-elimination proceeds normally (see the steps
\wn\mathsf{d}/\oc\mathsf{d}
and
\wn\mathsf{w}/\oc\mathsf{w}
).

2.

The step
\wn\mathsf{c}/\oc\mathsf{c}
is slightly more complex:
intuitively, it connects the dual premises of a
\wn
-contraction and of a
\oc
-contraction in all possible ways.

3.

The step
\wn\mathsf{c}/\oc\mathsf{w}
duplicates the rule
\oc\mathsf{w}
, spreading the information that there are no available resources to the premises of
\wn\mathsf{c}
.

4.

The step
\wn\mathsf{d}/\oc\mathsf{w}
represents a
mismatch
in supply and demand:
\wn
-dereliction asks for a resource but
\oc
-weakening says that it is not available;
the rule
\mathsf{zero}
in the resulting derivation keeps track of this mismatch, as a sort of error in computation, and ensures that the conclusion is preserved.

5.

In the step
\wn\mathsf{c}/\oc\mathsf{d}
,
\wn
-contraction represents two possible demands for a resource, but according to
\oc
-dereliction only one resource is available, so there is a
non-deterministic choice
on which request will be fed, the other one will receive a
\oc
-weakening; the rule
\mathsf{sum}
has to be intended as a way to keep track of all possible choices,
not
as a way to
duplicate resources
; said differently, in the step
\wn\mathsf{c}/\oc\mathsf{d}
a derivation reduces to a pair of derivations (of slices, if we consider their canonical forms).

By duality, the discussion above about resource management is similar for the steps
\wn\mathsf{w}/\oc\mathsf{c}
,
\wn\mathsf{w}/\oc\mathsf{d}
and
\wn\mathsf{d}/\oc\mathsf{c}
, respectively.

Figure

4
provides an example of the cut-elimination procedure in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
.

Figure 4:
An example of the cut-elimination procedure in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
sequent calculus.

It is worth comparing cut-elimination steps as defined for
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
sequent calculus (
Figure

3
) and for
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
interaction nets (
[
10
, Sect. 2]
,
[
29
, Fig. 4]
): symmetry and duality in the latter are lost in the former.

As there is no promotion rule
\mathsf{\oc p}
, in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
transforming a derivation in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
into one with atomic axioms does not commute with cut-elimination.
For instance, derivation
\pi
below reduces to
\pi^{\prime}
via cut-elimination;
but derivation
\pi_{\eta}
with atomic axioms, obtained from
\pi
through
\eta
-expansion (the procedure described in the proof of
Proposition

2.3
) reduces to
\pi_{\eta}^{\prime}\neq\pi^{\prime}
via cut-elimination.

\displaystyle\small\pi={\box_set_eq_clear:cN{g_{_{e}}bproof_{b}ox_{1}}\char 15\relax\hbox{$\vbox{}$}}\ \ \rightsquigarrow_{\mathsf{cut}}\ \ {\box_set_eq_clear:cN{g_{_{e}}bproof_{b}ox_{1}}\char 15\relax\hbox{$\vbox{}$}}=\pi^{\prime}\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\pi_{\eta}={\box_set_eq_clear:cN{g_{_{e}}bproof_{b}ox_{1}}\char 15\relax\hbox{$\vbox{}$}}\ \ \rightsquigarrow_{\mathsf{cut}}\ \ {\box_set_eq_clear:cN{g_{_{e}}bproof_{b}ox_{1}}\char 15\relax\hbox{$\vbox{}$}}=\pi_{\eta}^{\prime}

3
A Calculus of Structures for
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}

In this section,
we introduce a deep inference system
[
22
,
3
,
20
,
39
]
suitable for
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
, using the
open deduction
formalism
[
21
,
38
]
.
As a first novelty, we internalize the rules
\mathsf{zero}
and
\mathsf{sum}
of
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
sequent calculus at the level of formulas.
In fact, derivations in deep inference systems have a sequence structure instead of the more general tree-like structure of sequent calculus: every rule in deep inference has exactly one premise, consisting of one formula.
This because the meta-connectives for sequent composition (the comma) and sequent juxtaposition (derivation branching) are internalized by
\invamp
and
\otimes
, respectively.
To internalize the
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
meta-connective for sum, together with its unit, we introduce the (commutative and associative) binary connective
+
and its unit
\mathsf{0}
.
2
2

2

Here, the new connective
+
has nothing to do with the additive disjunction
\oplus
in
\mathsf{LL}
; and the unit
\mathsf{0}
for
+
must not be confused with the additive unit
0
for
\oplus
in
\mathsf{LL}
.

In this way, the rule
\mathsf{sum}
branches the derivation tree with a connective,
+
;
and similarly, the rule
\mathsf{zero}
has is own premise,
\mathsf{0}
.
Thus,
formulas
are defined by:

A,B\Coloneqq a\mid\bar{a}\mid A\otimes B\mid A\invamp B\mid{\mathsf{1}}\mid\mathsf{\bot}\mid\oc A\mid\wn B\mid\mathsf{0}\mid A+B

where
a,b,c,\dots
range over the usual countably infinite set of propositional
variables (so, a
\mathsf{MELL}
formula as defined on p.
2
is a formula with no occurrences of
+
and
\mathsf{0}
).
Formulas are identified up to the
equivalence

\simeq
defined as the least congruence on formulas generated by the relations in (
2
).

\small\begin{gathered}\begin{array}[]{c@{\quad\quad}c@{\quad\quad}c}A\invamp B\simeq B\invamp A\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt&A\otimes B\simeq B\otimes A\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt&A+B\simeq B+A\\
A\invamp(B\invamp C)\simeq(A\invamp B)\invamp C\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt&A\otimes(B\otimes C)\simeq(A\otimes B)\otimes C\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt&A+(B+C)\simeq(A+B)+C\\
A\invamp\mathsf{\bot}\simeq A\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt&A\otimes{\mathsf{1}}\simeq A\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt&A+\mathsf{0}\simeq A\end{array}\\[-2.0pt]
\begin{array}[]{c@{\qquad\qquad}c@{\qquad}c}A\invamp(B+C)\simeq(A\invamp B)+(A\invamp C)\hfil\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt&A\otimes(B+C)\simeq(A\otimes B)+(A\otimes C)\hfil\hskip 18.49988pt\\
\oc(A+B)\simeq\oc A+\oc B\hfil\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt&\wn(A+B)\simeq\wn A+\wn B\hfil\hskip 18.49988pt\\
\mathsf{0}\otimes A\simeq\mathsf{0}\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 9.24994pt\mathsf{0}\invamp A\simeq\mathsf{0}\hfil\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt&\wn\mathsf{0}\simeq\mathsf{0}\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 9.24994pt\oc\mathsf{0}\simeq\mathsf{0}\hfil\hskip 18.49988pt\end{array}\end{gathered}

(2)

Some equivalences in (
2
) correspond to well-known isomorphisms in
\mathsf{MLL}
.
With respect to
\simeq
, the formula

\mathsf{0}
is an annihilating element for all other connectives but
+
, for which it is a neutral element;
every connective other than
+
distributes over
+
.

An
additive normal
formula
A
is a sum of
\mathsf{MELL}
formulas,

i.e.

A=A_{1}+\cdots+A_{n}
(
n\in\mathbb{N}
) where all
A_{i}
’s are
\mathsf{MELL}
formulas (
A=\mathsf{0}
for
n=0
).
For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we set
\mathsf{n}=\underbrace{{\mathsf{1}}+\cdots+{\mathsf{1}}}_{n\textup{ times}}
.
Note that, by the equivalences in (
2
),
\mathsf{n}\otimes\mathsf{m}=\mathsf{k}
where
k=n\times m
.

A
context
(resp.
\mathsf{MELL}

context
)
\mathsf{C}\{\}
is a formula (resp.
\mathsf{MELL}
formula) with exactly one occurrence of the hole
\{\,\}
(which can be thought of as a special propositional variable).
We write
\mathsf{C}\{A\}
for the formula obtained from the context
\mathsf{C}\{\}
by replacing its hole with the formula
A
.

Remark 3.1

(Additive normal form)
.

By definition of
\simeq
, if
\mathsf{C}\{\}
is a context,
\mathsf{C}\{A+\mathsf{0}\}\simeq\mathsf{C}\{A\}
and
\mathsf{C}\{A+B\}\simeq\mathsf{C}\{A\}+\mathsf{C}\{B\}
. If
\mathsf{C}\{\}
is a
\mathsf{MELL}
context,
\mathsf{C}\{\mathsf{0}\}\simeq\mathsf{0}
.
In general, any formula
A
has an additive normal formula
A^{\prime}
such that
A^{\prime}\simeq A
.
Indeed,
equivalences in (
2
) but the ones on the
first line can be oriented to define a terminating rewriting relation whose normal forms are
additive normal
formulas.

Derivations.

We present deep inference derivations in the open deduction formalism, according to their “
synchronal
” form, where there is maximal parallelism between inference steps (see
[
21
,
38
]
).

A
deep inference system

\mathcal{S}
is a set of unary inference rules.
A
derivation

\mathcal{D}
from a
premise

B
to a
conclusion

A
in a deep inference system
\mathcal{S}
, noted

B

\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}\;

\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\;\mathcal{S}

A

or
\mathcal{D}\mathbin{\,\triangleright\,}B\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}}{{\hbox to12.58pt{\vbox to6.09pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.59999pt\lower-3.04544pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 15.75 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -3.94 L 0 3.94}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}A
, is defined as follows:

•

(
assumption
) a formula
A
is a derivation (denoted by
A
) with premise and conclusion
A
;

•

(
horizontal composition
)
if for all
i\in\{1,2\}

\mathcal{D}_{i}
is a derivation
from
B_{i}

to
A_{i}
,
then for any
\bullet\in\{\invamp,\otimes,+\}
,
\mathcal{D}_{1}\bullet\mathcal{D}_{2}
is a derivation
from
B_{1}\bullet B_{2}

to
A_{1}\bullet A_{2}
(see (
3
) below on the left);

•

(
vertical composition
) if

{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 6.9267pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 0.2934pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$A_{1}$}}}\kern 0.2934pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\rho\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to12.57294pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$B_{2}$}\kern 0.0pt$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\,\in\mathcal{S}

and, for all
i\in\{1,2\}
,
\mathcal{D}_{i}
is a derivation from
B_{i}
to
A_{i}
, then

\mathcal{D}_{1}\circ_{\mathsf{\rho}}\mathcal{D}_{2}
is a derivation from
B_{1}
to
A_{2}
(see (
3
) below on the right).
3
3

3

We can write

A

\scriptstyle\simeq\;

B

as a rule in a derivation if
A\simeq B
, although formally its use is implicit as formulas are identified up to
\simeq
.
Said differently, our formalism for derivations can be seen as a
calculus of structures
(in the sense of
[
38
]
) that extends the equivalence relation
\simeq
on formulas — defined in (
2
) — to an equivalence relation on derivations.

All rules in

The
down-fragment
and
up-fragment
4
4

4

Usually in the literature on deep inference, the dual rule
{\mathsf{r}}^{\uparrow}
of a rule
{\mathsf{r}}^{\downarrow}
is called “co-
\mathsf{r}
”.
We avoid these names because they clash with the usual terminology in the literature on
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
, see
Footnote

1
.

of
\mathsf{SDDI}
are the following sets of rules:

\begin{array}[]{c}\mathsf{DDI}^{\downarrow}=\{{\mathsf{ai}}^{\downarrow},\allowbreak{\mathsf{\oc d}}^{\downarrow},\allowbreak{\mathsf{\wn d}}^{\downarrow},{\mathsf{\oc w}}^{\downarrow},{\mathsf{\wn w}}^{\downarrow},{\mathsf{\oc c}}^{\downarrow},{\mathsf{\wn c}}^{\downarrow},{+}^{\downarrow},{\mathsf{0}}^{\downarrow},\mathsf{s}\}\qquad\mathsf{DDI}^{\uparrow}=\{{\mathsf{ai}}^{\uparrow},{\mathsf{\oc d}}^{\uparrow},{\mathsf{\wn d}}^{\uparrow},{\mathsf{\oc w}}^{\uparrow},\allowbreak{\mathsf{\wn w}}^{\uparrow},\allowbreak{\mathsf{\oc c}}^{\uparrow},\allowbreak{\mathsf{\wn c}}^{\uparrow},\allowbreak{+}^{\uparrow},{\mathsf{0}}^{\uparrow},\mathsf{s}\}\end{array}

Note the up/down symmetry between
\mathsf{DDI}^{\downarrow}
and
\mathsf{DDI}^{\uparrow}
, and that

\mathsf{SDDI}=\mathsf{DDI}^{\downarrow}\cup\mathsf{DDI}^{\uparrow}
with
\mathsf{DDI}^{\downarrow}\cap\mathsf{DDI}^{\uparrow}=\{\mathsf{s}\}
.
We set
\mathsf{DDI}^{\downarrow}_{-}=\mathsf{DDI}^{\downarrow}\setminus\{{+}^{\downarrow},{\mathsf{0}}^{\downarrow}\}
.
Note that in a
\mathsf{DDI}^{\downarrow}_{-}
derivation only
\mathsf{MELL}
formulas occur.

Roughly, rules in
\mathsf{DDI}^{\downarrow}
somehow mimic the ones in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}\setminus\{\mathsf{cut}\}
.
Rules in
\mathsf{DDI}^{\uparrow}
are their duals, turning them upside down.
Derivations in
\mathsf{DDI}^{\downarrow}_{-}
correspond to cut-free slices in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}^{-}

(see
Theorem

4.3
)
.

Remark 3.2

(Deep)
.

The idea of deep inference is that inference rules can be applied “deep” in any context:
in a deep inference system
\mathcal{S}
, if
{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 6.9267pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$B$}}}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{\rho}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to8.0868pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 0.2934pt\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 1.25pt\hbox{$A$}\kern 1.25pt$}}\kern 0.2934pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\in\mathcal{S}
then, for any context
\mathsf{C}\{\}
,

\mathsf{C}\{B\}

\scriptstyle\mathsf{\rho}\;

\mathsf{C}\{A\}

is derivable in
\mathcal{S}
.
Therefore, a derivation in
\mathcal{S}
can be seen as a finite
sequence
of “deep”
rules: for instance,
the derivation

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\framebox{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\vbox{\hbox{\kern 12.15079pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 2.184pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$a$}}}\kern 2.184pt}\kern 1.29166pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.31248pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{\wn d}}^{\downarrow}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to9.26834pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.31248pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}}\kern 1.29166pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\wn a$}\kern 0.0pt$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}$}}}\otimes{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\framebox{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\vbox{\hbox{\kern 10.72163pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 1.28471pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$b$}}}\kern 1.28471pt}\kern 1.29166pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.31248pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{\oc d}}^{\downarrow}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to6.5333pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.31248pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}}\kern 1.29166pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 1.35832pt\hbox{$\oc b$}\kern 1.35832pt$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}$}}}

in
\mathsf{DDI}^{\downarrow}
(with parallel
{\mathsf{\wn d}}^{\downarrow}
and
{\mathsf{\oc d}}^{\downarrow}
) can be “sequenced” as both

\box_set_eq_clear:cN
g˙˙ebproof˙box˙1ﬄ

and

\box_set_eq_clear:cN
g˙˙ebproof˙box˙1ﬄ
.
Often we implicitly identify a derivation in a deep inference system
\mathcal{S}
with its sequenced presentations.

Indeed,

System
\mathsf{SDDI}
has only the
atomic
introduction rules
{\mathsf{ai}}^{\downarrow}
and
{\mathsf{ai}}^{\uparrow}
(indeed
a
is a propositional variable in
Figure

5
): they can be seen as the atomic version of
\mathsf{ax}
- and
\mathsf{cut}
-rules of sequent calculus, respectively.
The
non-atomic
versions of the rules
{\mathsf{ai}}^{\downarrow}
and
{\mathsf{ai}}^{\uparrow}
are respectively:

\qquad{}\smash{{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 8.63052pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 8.61108pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{${\mathsf{1}}$}}}\kern 8.61108pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{i}}^{\downarrow}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to22.2222pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$A\invamp\bar{A}$}\kern 0.0pt$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}}\qquad\qquad\qquad{}\smash{{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 8.63052pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$A\otimes\bar{A}$}}}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{i}}^{\uparrow}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to22.5pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 7.3611pt\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 1.1111pt\hbox{$\mathsf{\bot}$}\kern 1.1111pt$}}\kern 7.3611pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}}\qquad\qquad\qquad\text{(where }A\text{ is a $\mathsf{MELL}$ formula)}

However, the rules
{\mathsf{i}}^{\downarrow}
and
{\mathsf{i}}^{\uparrow}
are derivable in
\mathsf{SDDI}
(
Lemma

3.4
).
Derivability of
{\mathsf{i}}^{\downarrow}
is analogous to the fact that a derivation can be transformed to one with atomic axioms in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
sequent calculus (
Proposition

2.3
), but derivability of
{\mathsf{i}}^{\uparrow}
is a typical result in deep inference systems that does not have a corresponding result in the sequent calculus: it says that restricting cuts to an
atomic
level is not limiting.

Lemma 3.4

(Atomic axioms and atomic cuts)
.

The rule
{\mathsf{i}}^{\downarrow}
is derivable in
\{{\mathsf{ai}}^{\downarrow},\mathsf{s},{\mathsf{\wn d}}^{\downarrow},{\mathsf{\oc d}}^{\downarrow}\}
;
and the rule
{\mathsf{i}}^{\uparrow}
is derivable in
\{{\mathsf{ai}}^{\uparrow},\mathsf{s},{\mathsf{\wn d}}^{\uparrow},{\mathsf{\oc d}}^{\uparrow}\}
.

∎

The rule
{\mathsf{i}}^{\uparrow}
plays a special role in deep inference systems, as the
\mathsf{cut}
does in sequent calculi.
Thanks to
\simeq
and
{\mathsf{0}}^{\downarrow}
, it makes superfluous all the rules in
\mathsf{DDI}^{\uparrow}
(second line in
Figure

5
) but
{\mathsf{0}}^{\uparrow}
and
\mathsf{s}
.
Note that
{\mathsf{ai}}^{\uparrow}
is not enough for that, because
{\mathsf{i}}^{\uparrow}
needs
{\mathsf{\oc d}}^{\uparrow}
and
{\mathsf{\wn d}}^{\uparrow}
to be simulated by
{\mathsf{ai}}^{\uparrow}
, as seen in
Lemma

3.4
.

Lemma 3.5

(Getting rid of up-rules via
{\mathsf{i}}^{\uparrow}
and
{\mathsf{0}}^{\downarrow}
)
.

Any rule
{\mathsf{\rho}}^{\uparrow}\!\in\!\{{\mathsf{\oc d}}^{\uparrow},{\mathsf{\wn d}}^{\uparrow},{\mathsf{\oc c}}^{\uparrow},{\mathsf{\wn c}}^{\uparrow},{\mathsf{\oc w}}^{\uparrow},{\mathsf{\wn w}}^{\uparrow}\}
is derivable
in
\{{\mathsf{\rho}}^{\downarrow},{\mathsf{i}}^{\uparrow},{\mathsf{i}}^{\downarrow},\mathsf{s}\}
;
the rule
{+}^{\uparrow}
is derivable in
\{{\mathsf{0}}^{\downarrow}\}
.

Proof.

For a rule

\bar{B}

\scriptstyle{\mathsf{\rho}}^{\uparrow}\;

\bar{A}

with

{\mathsf{\rho}}^{\uparrow}\in\{{\mathsf{\oc d}}^{\uparrow},{\mathsf{\wn d}}^{\uparrow},{\mathsf{\oc c}}^{\uparrow},{\mathsf{\wn c}}^{\uparrow},{\mathsf{\oc w}}^{\uparrow},{\mathsf{\wn w}}^{\uparrow}\}
, see (
4
).
For the rule

A

\scriptstyle{+}^{\uparrow}\;

A+A

, see (
5
).

\bar{B}

\scriptstyle\simeq\;

{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\framebox{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\vbox{\hbox{\kern 6.755pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 8.05832pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{${\mathsf{1}}$}}}\kern 8.05832pt}\kern 1.29166pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.31248pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{i}}^{\downarrow}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to20.74161pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.31248pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}}\kern 1.29166pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\bar{A}\invamp A$}\kern 0.0pt$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}$}}}\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\kern 0.4pt\hbox{\kern 0.4pt$\vbox{\hbox{}}$\kern 0.4pt}\kern 0.4pt}}$}}\otimes\bar{B}

\scriptstyle\mathsf{s}\;

\bar{A}\invamp{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\framebox{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\vbox{\hbox{\kern 6.755pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{${{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\framebox{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\vbox{\hbox{\kern 9.27574pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 0.25572pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$A$}}}\kern 0.25572pt}\kern 1.29166pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.31248pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{\rho}}^{\downarrow}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to7.4475pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.31248pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}}\kern 1.29166pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.90121pt\hbox{$B$}\kern 0.90121pt$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}$}}}\otimes\bar{B}$}}}}\kern 1.29166pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.31248pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{i}}^{\uparrow}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to154.6923pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.31248pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.29166pt\hbox{\kern 73.74895pt\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 1.02777pt\hbox{$\mathsf{\bot}$}\kern 1.02777pt$}}\kern 73.74895pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}$}}}\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\kern 0.4pt\hbox{\kern 0.4pt$\vbox{\hbox{}}$\kern 0.4pt}\kern 0.4pt}}$}}

\scriptstyle\simeq\;

\bar{A}

(4)

A

\scriptstyle\simeq\;

{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\kern 0.4pt\hbox{\kern 0.4pt$\vbox{\hbox{\kern 10.4583pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 1.25pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\mathsf{0}$}}}\kern 1.25pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{0}}^{\downarrow}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to7.50002pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 1.25pt\hbox{$A$}\kern 1.25pt$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}$\kern 0.4pt}\kern 0.4pt}}$}}+A

(5)

∎

4
Correspondence between
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
and
\mathsf{SDDI}

In this section we prove that
\mathsf{SDDI}
is a
sound
and
complete
proof system for
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}

sequent calculus.
At first sight, this result is obvious because the rules
\mathsf{zero}
in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
and
{\mathsf{0}}^{\downarrow}
in
\mathsf{SDDI}
make everything provable.
But the interest is to show that the fragments without
\mathsf{zero}
and
{\mathsf{0}}^{\downarrow}
correspond to each other.

If
\Gamma=A_{1},\dots,A_{n}
(with
n\in\mathbb{N}
) is a
\mathsf{MELL}
sequent, we set
\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket=A_{1}\invamp\cdots\invamp A_{n}
(so,
\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket=\bot
for
n=0
).

{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 9.71585pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$$}}}\kern 9.71585pt}\kern 1.14815pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.12503pt\hbox{$$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to19.4317pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.12503pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\;\mathsf{ax}$}}$}\hss}}\kern 1.14815pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\vdash a,\bar{a}$}\kern 0.0pt$}}}}}\kern 7.49556pt}}}\overset{\llbracket\,\rrbracket}{\to}\ {\vbox{\hbox{\kern 8.7511pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 6.57687pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{${\mathsf{1}}$}}}\kern 6.57687pt}\kern 1.14815pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.12503pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{ai}}^{\downarrow}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to17.40381pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.12503pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}}\kern 1.14815pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$a\invamp\bar{a}$}\kern 0.0pt$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}

) for some

, and
{\mathsf{1}}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{SDDI}}}{{\hbox to12.58pt{\vbox to6.09pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.59999pt\lower-3.04544pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

.
Moreover,

\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}^{-}}}{{\hbox to12.58pt{\vbox to6.09pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.59999pt\lower-3.04544pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

then
{\mathsf{1}}\!\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{DDI}^{\downarrow}_{-}\cup\{{\mathsf{i}}^{\uparrow}\}}}{{\hbox to12.58pt{\vbox to6.09pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.59999pt\lower-3.04544pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}\!\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket
;
if

then
{\mathsf{1}}\!\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{DDI}^{\downarrow}_{-}}}{{\hbox to12.58pt{\vbox to6.09pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.59999pt\lower-3.04544pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}

;
if

then

.

Proof.

If we show that
\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}}}{{\hbox to12.58pt{\vbox to6.09pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.59999pt\lower-3.04544pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 15.75 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -3.94 L 0 3.94}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}\Gamma
implies
\mathsf{n}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{DDI}^{\downarrow}\cup\{{\mathsf{i}}^{\uparrow}\}}}{{\hbox to12.58pt{\vbox to6.09pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.59999pt\lower-3.04544pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 15.75 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -3.94 L 0 3.94}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket
, then
\mathsf{n}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{SDDI}}}{{\hbox to12.58pt{\vbox to6.09pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.59999pt\lower-3.04544pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 15.75 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -3.94 L 0 3.94}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket
by
Lemma

3.4
, and thus
{\mathsf{1}}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{SDDI}}}{{\hbox to12.58pt{\vbox to6.09pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.59999pt\lower-3.04544pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 15.75 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -3.94 L 0 3.94}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket
by
Remark

3.3
.
So, let
\pi
be a derivation of
\vdash\!\Gamma
in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
.
By
Proposition

2.3
we can assume that
\pi
is
\eta
-expanded.
By induction on
\pi
, we define a derivation
\llbracket\pi\rrbracket\mathbin{\,\triangleright\,}\mathsf{n}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{DDI}^{\downarrow}\cup\{{\mathsf{i}}^{\uparrow}\}}}{{\hbox to12.58pt{\vbox to6.09pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.59999pt\lower-3.04544pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 15.75 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -3.94 L 0 3.94}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket

(for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
) as shown in
Figure

6
.
According to this translation, if
\pi
is in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}\setminus\{\mathsf{cut}\}
(resp. in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}^{-}\setminus\{\mathsf{cut}\}
; in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}^{-}
; in
\{\mathsf{zero}\}
) then
\llbracket\pi\rrbracket
is in
\mathsf{DDI}^{\downarrow}
(resp. in
\mathsf{DDI}^{\downarrow}_{-}
and
n=1
; in
\mathsf{DDI}^{\downarrow}_{-}\cup\{{\mathsf{i}}^{\uparrow}\}
and
n=1
; in
\{{\mathsf{0}}^{\downarrow}\}
and
n=0
).
∎

Completeness (
Theorem

4.1
) says that slices of a derivation in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
(
i.e.
derivations in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}^{-}
) with atomic axioms correspond to derivations in
\mathsf{DDI}^{\downarrow}_{-}\cup\{{\mathsf{i}}^{\uparrow}\}
(and so in
\mathsf{SDDI}\setminus\{{+}^{\uparrow},{\mathsf{0}}^{\uparrow},{+}^{\downarrow},{\mathsf{0}}^{\downarrow}\}
, by rewriting
{\mathsf{i}}^{\uparrow}
according to
Lemma

3.4
) with only
\mathsf{MELL}
formulas, via the translation
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
defined in

Figure

6
.
Soundness (
Theorem

4.2
) says somehow that the converse holds too.

Proof.

Clearly, for any
\mathsf{MELL}
sequent
\Gamma
, there is a derivation in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
sequent calculus:

\box_set_eq_clear:cN
g˙˙ebproof˙box˙1ﬄ
.

Let us assume that we have a derivation
\mathcal{D}
in
\mathsf{DDI}^{\downarrow}_{-}\cup\{{\mathsf{i}}^{\uparrow}\}
from
{\mathsf{1}}
to
\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket
.
To define the derivation of
\vdash\Gamma
in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}^{-}
, we consider the formulas occurring in
\mathcal{D}
(which actually are
\mathsf{MELL}
formulas) not up to
\simeq
, so when
\simeq
is required, its use is made explicit as if it were an inference rule (see also
Footnote

3
).
For any
\mathsf{\rho}\in\mathsf{DDI}^{\downarrow}_{-}\cup\{{\mathsf{i}}^{\uparrow},\simeq\}
, if

B

\scriptstyle\mathsf{\rho}\;

A

then
\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}^{-}}}{{\hbox to12.58pt{\vbox to6.09pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.59999pt\lower-3.04544pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 15.75 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -3.94 L 0 3.94}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}\overline{B},A
as shown in
Figure

7
.
Since
A
and
B
are
\mathsf{MELL}
formulas, many equivalences in (
2
) cannot occur in
\mathcal{D}
.
The cases for
\mathsf{\rho}={\simeq}
corresponding to
A\otimes B\simeq B\otimes A
and
A\otimes(B\otimes C)\simeq(A\otimes B)\otimes C
and
A\otimes{\mathsf{1}}\simeq A
are omitted in
Figure

7
as they are analogous to the ones for
\invamp
.

Consider
\mathcal{D}
as sequenced (
Remark

3.2
).
By induction on the
\mathsf{MELL}
context
\mathsf{C}\{\}
, we prove that if

\mathsf{C}\{B\}

\scriptstyle\rho\;

\mathsf{C}\{A\}

occurs in
\mathcal{D}
,
then
\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}^{-}}}{{\hbox to12.58pt{\vbox to6.09pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.59999pt\lower-3.04544pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 15.75 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -3.94 L 0 3.94}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}\overline{\mathsf{C}\{B\}},\mathsf{C}\{A\}
.
We have just shown the case
\mathsf{C}\{\}=\{
}.
Other cases:

\small\begin{array}[]{c@{\qquad}|@{\qquad}c}\mathsf{C}\{A\}=D\invamp\mathsf{C}^{\prime}\{A\}\text{ (or similarly }\mathsf{C}\{A\}=D\otimes\mathsf{C}^{\prime}\{A\}\text{)}\hfil\hskip 18.49988pt\vrule\hskip 18.49988pt&\mathsf{C}\{A\}=\oc\mathsf{C}^{\prime}\{A\}\text{ (or smilarly }\mathsf{C}\{A\}=\wn\mathsf{C}^{\prime}\{A\}\text{)}\\

Let us sum up the correspondence between

sequent calculus and
\mathsf{SDDI}
deep inference system.

Theorem 4.3

(Sequent calculus vs. deep inference)
.

be a

sequent.

1.

\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}

:

if and only if
{\mathsf{1}}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{SDDI}}}{{\hbox to12.58pt{\vbox to6.09pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.59999pt\lower-3.04544pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

then
{\mathsf{1}}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{DDI}^{\downarrow}_{-}}}{{\hbox to12.58pt{\vbox to6.09pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.59999pt\lower-3.04544pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 15.75 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -3.94 L 0 3.94}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket
.

Proof.

1.

For
\Rightarrow
, by completeness (
Theorem

4.1
); for
\Leftarrow
, by soundness (
Theorem

4.2
).

2.–3.

For
\Rightarrow
, see
Theorem

4.1
.
For
\Leftarrow
(only for
Item

2
), by
Theorems

2.4
and
4.3
.
1
.
∎

5
Normalization in
\mathsf{SDDI}

In this section we define a
standard
form for derivations in
\mathsf{SDDI}
and a
normalization
procedure to obtain a “cut-free” standard derivation in
\mathsf{DDI}^{\downarrow}
for any formula
A
provable in
\mathsf{SDDI}
.
The usual approach to prove normalization in deep inference system relies on the

splitting technique

[
20
,
24
,
2
,
23
,
33
]
.
However, the presence in our syntax of the connective
+
and its unit
\mathsf{0}
prevents us to use Guglielmi and Tubella’s normalization result
[
37
,
38
]
,
which covers and generalizes the splitting proofs.
This is mainly due to the fact that
\mathsf{0}
is an absorbing element for
\otimes
,
\invamp
,
\wn
and
\oc
, together with the distributivity over
+
.

For this reason, following
[
33
,
34
]
,
we define the normalization process in terms of
rule permutations
, which play the same role as cut-elimination steps in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
.
In some cases, their definition relies on the rules for the connective
+
and its unit
\mathsf{0}
. This behavior is coherent with the dynamics of cut-elimination in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}

[
10
,
29
,
13
]
, where the rules
\mathsf{sum}
and
\mathsf{zero}
step in to deal with non-deterministic choices or mismatches between “supply and demand” (see
Section

2
).
Interestingly, these permutations in
\mathsf{SDDI}
mimic the elegant symmetries of cut-elimination steps as defined for interaction nets
[
29
, Fig. 4]
, instead of the awkward rewrite rules defined for the sequent calculus (
Figure

3
).

The fact that the syntax for
\mathsf{SDDI}
is more flexible and symmetric than the sequent calculus, and internalizes the connective
+
and its unit
\mathsf{0}
, allows for a more fine-grained analysis of the normalization process than in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
.
In particular, we can distinguish three kinds of rule permutations corresponding to three distinct phases in normalizing:
\mathsf{MLL}

cut-elimination steps
(involving
{\mathsf{ai}}^{\uparrow}
,
{\mathsf{ai}}^{\downarrow}
and
\mathsf{s}
only),
resource management steps
(involving the
\wn
- and the
\oc
-rules only) and
slice operations
(the process of duplicating or removing a slice, which is less evident in the
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
sequent calculus and
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
interaction nets).

Definition 5.1

(Permutation)
.

In
\mathsf{SDDI}
, a rule
\mathsf{\rho}

permutes over
a rule
\mathsf{\sigma}
(or
\mathsf{\sigma}

permutes under

\mathsf{\rho}
) if,
for any derivation

C

\scriptstyle\mathsf{\sigma}\;

B

\scriptstyle\mathsf{\rho}\;

A

, one of the following holds:

\begin{array}[]{c@{\; ; \myskip\!\!\!\!\!\!}c@{\; ; \myskip\!\!\!\!}c@{\; ; \myskip\!\!\!\!}c@{\; ; \myskip\!\!\!\!\!}c}\quad{\small A=C}\hfil\;;\hskip 30.00005pt&\quad{\small{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 7.01425pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$C$}}}}\kern 1.29166pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.31248pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{\sigma}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to7.24286pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.31248pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.29166pt\hbox{\kern 6.52574pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$C$}}}}\kern 1.29166pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.31248pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{\rho}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to7.24286pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.31248pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.29166pt\hbox{\kern }B^{\prime}\hfil\;;\hskip 30.00005pt&{\small{}\smash{{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 18.49785pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 1.5044pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$C$}}}\kern 1.5044pt}\kern to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.31248pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.29166pt\hbox{\kern 20.15564pt\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 1.15694pt\hbox{$A$}\kern 1.15694pt$}}\kern 1.65779pt}}}\kern A rule
\mathsf{\rho}

permutes over
a rule
\mathsf{\sigma}
(or
\mathsf{\sigma}

permutes under

\mathsf{\rho}
)
by
a set of rules

\mathsf{\sigma}

0.0pt}}}\kern-0.64583pt\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 5.81435pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}}\vbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{$\vbox to8.0pt{\hbox{\vrule width=0.6pt,height=8.0pt\kern 1.2pt\vrule width=0.6pt,height=8.0pt}}$}}}$}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\;\mathcal{S}$}\hss}\kern 2.93576pt}\kern-0.64583pt\hbox{\kern 5.81435pt\hbox{\kern 1.20001pt}\kern 2.93576pt}}}\kern 0.06424pt}}}\kern 0.64583pt\hbox{\kern 3.3117pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}}\vbox{\hbox{\hfil}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}\hss}\kern 0.49736pt}\kern 0.64583pt\hbox{\kern 3.3117pt\hbox{\hbox{$B^{\prime}$}}\kern 0.49736pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\kern 0.64583pt\hbox{\kern 3.3117pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 1.125pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{\sigma}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to5.0053pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 1.125pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 0.49736pt}\kern 0.64583pt\hbox{\kern 4.12685pt\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.5625pt\hbox{$A$}\kern 0.5625pt$}}\kern 1.3125pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}}$}}}\quad\mbox{ for some formulas }B^{\prime},B^{\prime\prime}\hfil\;.\hskip 15.00002pt\end{array}

Roughly, permuting
\mathsf{\sigma}
under
\mathsf{\rho}
means that
\mathsf{\sigma}
can be pushed below
\mathsf{\rho}
in a derivation with same premise and conclusion.
In this operation,
\mathsf{\rho}
or
\mathsf{\sigma}
might disappear or other rules might appear in between.
The definition of rule permutation is asymmetric:
two
\rho
’s can be above one
\sigma
, but not two
\sigma
’s below one
\rho
.

We call
trivial
the rule permutations identified by the open deduction syntax, such as the one below.

\small{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 9.27574pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$B_{1}\otimes B_{2}$}}}}\kern 1.29166pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.31248pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{\rho}_{1}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to32.84482pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.31248pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.29166pt\hbox{\kern 0.25572pt\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$A_{1}\otimes B_{2}$}\kern 0.0pt$}}\kern 0.25572pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\kern 1.29166pt\hbox{\kern 9.53146pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.31248pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{\rho}_{2}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to32.33337pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.31248pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 0.25572pt}\kern 1.29166pt\hbox{\kern 9.78719pt\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$A_{1}\otimes A_{2}$}\kern 0.0pt$}}\kern 0.51144pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}={{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\framebox{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\vbox{\hbox{\kern 9.27574pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$B_{1}$}}}}\kern 1.29166pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.31248pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{\rho}_{1}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to11.79744pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.31248pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.29166pt\hbox{\kern to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to11.79744pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.31248pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.29166pt\hbox{\kern to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.31248pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{\rho}_{2}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to32.84482pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.31248pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.29166pt\hbox{\kern 0.25572pt\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$B_{1}\otimes A_{2}$}\kern 0.0pt$}}\kern 0.25572pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\kern 1.29166pt\hbox{\kern 9.53146pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.31248pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{\rho}_{1}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to32.33337pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.31248pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 0.25572pt}\kern 1.29166pt\hbox{\kern 9.78719pt\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$A_{1}\otimes A_{2}$}\kern 0.0pt$}}\kern 0.51144pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}

The following lemma is analogous to canonicity (
2.2
) for
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
sequent calculus.
It means that, in
\mathsf{SDDI}
, rules
{\mathsf{0}}^{\downarrow}
and
{+}^{\downarrow}
can be pushed down in a derivation, and rules
{\mathsf{0}}^{\uparrow}
and
{+}^{\uparrow}
can be pushed up.

Lemma 5.2

(Permuting
\mathsf{0}
and
+
)
.

Any rule in
\mathsf{SDDI}
permutes over
{\mathsf{0}}^{\downarrow}
and
{+}^{\downarrow}
, and under
{+}^{\uparrow}
and
{\mathsf{0}}^{\uparrow}
.

Proof.

We define the rule permutations below, for
\mathsf{\rho},\mathsf{\tau},\mathsf{\sigma}\in\mathsf{SDDI}
with
\mathsf{\rho}\neq{+}^{\uparrow}
,
\mathsf{\tau}\neq{\mathsf{0}}^{\uparrow}
and
\mathsf{\sigma}\not\in\{{+}^{\downarrow},{\mathsf{0}}^{\downarrow}\}
.

(6)

Note that some of the rule permutations in (
6
) may implicitly use formula equivalence
\simeq
in order to be applied, for example in the following rule permutations concerning
{\mathsf{ai}}^{\downarrow}
and
{+}^{\uparrow}
, or
{\mathsf{ai}}^{\downarrow}
and
{\mathsf{0}}^{\uparrow}
:

(7)

Similar permutations take place when
{\mathsf{ai}}^{\downarrow}
is replaced with
{\mathsf{\oc c}}^{\uparrow}
or
{\mathsf{\wn c}}^{\uparrow}
, and in their dual configurations.
∎

As a consequence of
Lemma

5.2
, pushing up the rules
{\mathsf{0}}^{\uparrow}
,
{+}^{\uparrow}
and down
{\mathsf{0}}^{\downarrow}
,
{+}^{\downarrow}
can be interpreted as
slice management
operations: it duplicates and discards the “slices” (the subderivations without the rule
{\mathsf{0}}^{\uparrow}
,
{+}^{\uparrow}
,
{\mathsf{0}}^{\downarrow}
,
{+}^{\downarrow}
) and extends them as much as possible, propagating the non-deterministic choice
+
and the resource mismatch
\mathsf{0}
all along the derivation.
It generalizes
Remark

3.1
(for formulas) to derivations.

Corollary 5.3

(Slice management)
.

Let
A
,
A^{\prime}
,
B
,
B^{\prime}
be
\mathsf{MELL}
formulas, and
\mathsf{C}\{\,\}
be a
\mathsf{MELL}
context.
Let
\rightsquigarrow_{\mathsf{norm}}
be one of the steps defined in (
6
), and
\rightsquigarrow_{\mathsf{norm}}^{n}
be a sequence of
n\in\mathbb{N}
of such steps.
Then,

where
m=|\mathcal{D}_{1}|+|\mathcal{D}_{2}|
and
k=|\mathcal{D}_{A}|+|\mathcal{D}_{B}|
(
|\mathcal{D}|
is the number of inference rules in the derivation
\mathcal{D}
).

Actually, we can further structure
\mathsf{SDDI}
derivations so as to separate an initial up-segment and a final down-segment (
Theorem

5.7
).
To prove this, we use the two following lemmas.

{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 9.03998pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 1.88542pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$A$}}}\kern 1.88542pt}\kern 1.00462pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.04861pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{\wn d}}^{\downarrow}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to9.78477pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.04861pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}}\kern 1.00462pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\wn A$}\kern 0.0pt$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\kern 1.00462pt\hbox{\kern 9.03998pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.04861pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{\oc d}}^{\uparrow}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to9.78477pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.04861pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.00462pt\hbox{\kern 10.9254pt\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 1.09027pt\hbox{$A$}\kern 1.09027pt$}}\kern 1.88542pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\rightsquigarrow_{\mathsf{norm}}{A\vbox{\hbox{}}}

{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 0.95277pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 8.0872pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 1.13194pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$A$}}}\kern 1.13194pt}\kern 1.00462pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.04861pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{\oc d}}^{\downarrow}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to8.27782pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.04861pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}}\kern 1.00462pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\oc A$}\kern 0.0pt$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\kern 1.00462pt\hbox{\kern 8.0872pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.04861pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{\wn d}}^{\uparrow}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to8.27782pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.04861pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.00462pt\hbox{\kern 9.21915pt\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 1.09027pt\hbox{$A$}\kern 1.09027pt$}}\kern 1.13194pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\rightsquigarrow_{\mathsf{norm}}{A\vbox{\hbox{}}}

\oc
and

by
\{{\mathsf{0}}^{\uparrow},{\mathsf{0}}^{\downarrow},{+}^{\uparrow},{+}^{\downarrow}\}
in
\mathsf{SDDI}
.

Lemma 5.4

(Permutations of rules for
\oc
and
\wn
)
.

In
\mathsf{SDDI}
, the following rule permutations hold:

1.

Interaction-net permutations:
The rules in
\{{\mathsf{\oc d}}^{\uparrow},{\mathsf{\wn d}}^{\uparrow},{\mathsf{\oc w}}^{\uparrow},{\mathsf{\wn w}}^{\uparrow},{\mathsf{\oc c}}^{\uparrow},{\mathsf{\wn c}}^{\uparrow}\}
permute over the rules in
\{{\mathsf{\oc d}}^{\downarrow},{\mathsf{\wn d}}^{\downarrow},{\mathsf{\oc w}}^{\downarrow},{\mathsf{\wn w}}^{\downarrow},{\mathsf{\oc c}}^{\downarrow},{\mathsf{\wn c}}^{\downarrow}\}
by the rules in
\{{+}^{\downarrow},{+}^{\uparrow},{\mathsf{0}}^{\downarrow},{\mathsf{0}}^{\uparrow}\}
;

2.

The rules in
\{{\mathsf{\wn d}}^{\downarrow},{\mathsf{\oc d}}^{\downarrow},{\mathsf{\wn w}}^{\downarrow},{\mathsf{\oc w}}^{\downarrow},{\mathsf{\wn c}}^{\downarrow},{\mathsf{\oc c}}^{\downarrow}\}
permutes under any rule in
\{{\mathsf{ai}}^{\uparrow},{\mathsf{ai}}^{\downarrow},\mathsf{s}\}
;

3.

The rules in
\{{\mathsf{\wn d}}^{\uparrow},{\mathsf{\oc d}}^{\uparrow},{\mathsf{\wn w}}^{\uparrow},{\mathsf{\oc w}}^{\uparrow},{\mathsf{\wn c}}^{\uparrow},{\mathsf{\oc c}}^{\uparrow}\}
permutes over any rule in
\{{\mathsf{ai}}^{\uparrow},{\mathsf{ai}}^{\downarrow},\mathsf{s}\}
.

Proof.

1.

By the (non-trivial) rule permutations in
Figure

8
or by their duals obtained by up/down symmetry.

2.

First, note that all rule permutations involving
{\mathsf{ai}}^{\downarrow}
in
\{{\mathsf{\wn d}}^{\downarrow},{\mathsf{\oc d}}^{\downarrow},{\mathsf{\wn w}}^{\downarrow},{\mathsf{\oc w}}^{\downarrow},\allowbreak{\mathsf{\wn c}}^{\downarrow},{\mathsf{\oc c}}^{\downarrow}\}
are trivial.
Moreover, any
\rho\in\{{\mathsf{\wn d}}^{\downarrow},{\mathsf{\oc d}}^{\downarrow},{\mathsf{\wn w}}^{\downarrow},{\mathsf{\oc w}}^{\downarrow},\allowbreak{\mathsf{\wn c}}^{\downarrow},{\mathsf{\oc c}}^{\downarrow}\}
permutes under
\mathsf{s}
as follows.

{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\kern 0.4pt\hbox{\kern 0.4pt$\vbox{\hbox{\kern 5.46104pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{${{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\framebox{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$\vbox{\hbox{\kern 6.9267pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$A^{\prime}$}}}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{\rho}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to10.30557pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 1.40277pt\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 1.25pt\hbox{$A$}\kern 1.25pt$}}\kern 1.40277pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}$}}}\otimes{\mathopen{}\left({B\invamp C}\right)\mathclose{}}$}}}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower (8)

We conclude by the following rule permutations for
{\mathsf{\wn d}}^{\downarrow}
,
{\mathsf{\wn c}}^{\downarrow}
and
{\mathsf{\wn w}}^{\downarrow}
(permutations for
{\mathsf{\oc d}}^{\downarrow}
,

and
{\mathsf{\oc w}}^{\downarrow}
are defined similarly).

3.

It can be obtained dually from
Item

2
, by the up/down symmetry of rules.
∎

Permutations in
Figure

8
and their duals correspond to the cut-elimination steps for modalities
\wn
and
\oc
in the
interaction-nets
presentation of
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
, see
[
29
, Fig. 4]
and
[
10
,
35
,
13
]
.
They take place when a down-rule for
\oc
meets an up-rule for
\wn
, or vice-versa, and deal with the
resource management
(see
Section

2
).
Akin to interaction-nets and unlike the sequent calculus, these permutations on
\mathsf{SDDI}
are perfectly symmetric.
Note the key role of the rules
{+}^{\uparrow}
,
{+}^{\downarrow}
,
{\mathsf{0}}^{\uparrow}
,
{\mathsf{0}}^{\downarrow}
in some permutations. In particular,

•

formulas
\mathsf{0}
appear when there is a mismatch between “supply and demand” (
{\mathsf{\wn w}}^{\downarrow}/{\mathsf{\oc d}}^{\uparrow}
and
{\mathsf{\oc w}}^{\downarrow}/{\mathsf{\wn d}}^{\uparrow}
),

•

formulas with
+
appear when there is a non-deterministic choice on which request will be fed (
{\mathsf{\wn c}}^{\downarrow}/{\mathsf{\oc d}}^{\uparrow}
and
{\mathsf{\oc c}}^{\downarrow}/{\mathsf{\wn d}}^{\uparrow}
).

Proof (sketch).

This is a standard result in deep inference systems.
Nowadays, it is usually proved via splitting
[
38
,
37
,
39
]
, as it is a consequence of cut-elimination, but the hypotheses to apply the splitting technique do not hold in
\mathsf{SDDI}
.
In
[
19
,
33
,
34
,
20
]
, which were written before the splitting technique was found,
Items

1
and
2
are proved using some sort of rule permutations.
To prove
Item

1
it is enough to use the non-trivial rule permutations of
{\mathsf{ai}}^{\downarrow}
over
\mathsf{s}
shown in (
9
) below, and the dual rule permutations of
{\mathsf{ai}}^{\uparrow}
under
\mathsf{s}
obtained from (
9
) by up/down symmetry.

\small{\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\kern 0.4pt\hbox{\kern 0.4pt$\vbox{\hbox{\kern 4.915pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 172.43752pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{${\mathsf{1}}\otimes{\mathopen{}\left({B\invamp Item

1
, if

then
A
is provable by starting from shallow
{\mathsf{ai}}^{\downarrow}
rules;
and if there is a rule
{\mathsf{ai}}^{\uparrow}
,
then there is at least one
{\mathsf{ai}}^{\uparrow}
that can be permuted up in the derivation,
until we can obtain a configuration as the one above on the right,
which can be replaced by a rule
{\mathsf{ai}}^{\downarrow}
.
∎

Rule permutations involved in the proof of
Lemma

5.5
.
2

essentially correspond to
\mathsf{MLL}
cut-elimination steps
in the
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
sequent calculus, indeed modalities
\oc
and
\wn
do not play any active role there.

Definition 5.6

(Normalization step)
.

Any rewrite relation on
\mathsf{SDDI}
derivations that is a non-trivial rule permutation used in the proofs of

Lemmas

5.2
,
5.4
and
5.5
is a
normalization step
and denoted by
\rightsquigarrow_{\mathsf{norm}}
.

Normalization steps
rearrange rules in a
\mathsf{DDI}^{\downarrow}
or
\mathsf{SDDI}
derivation in a fixed order, leaving unchanged its premise and conclusion.
So, derivability in
\mathsf{DDI}^{\downarrow}
and
\mathsf{SDDI}
can be decomposed in several segments.
More precisely, every derivation in
\mathsf{SDDI}
can be rearranged in a symmetrical way so that:

1.

on the top there is an
up-segment
where:

(a)

the first part consists of rules
{\mathsf{0}}^{\uparrow}
and
{+}^{\uparrow}
, which decompose the derivation in vertical slices;

(b)

the second part consists of up-rules for
\oc
and
\wn
, which deal with non-linear resources;

2.

in the middle there is a
linear segment
, roughly corresponding to
\mathsf{MLL}
and to linear resources;

3.

on the bottom there is a
down-segment
where:

(a)

the first part consists of down-rules for
\oc
and
\wn
, which deal with non-linear resources;

(b)

the second part consists of rules
{\mathsf{0}}^{\downarrow}
and
{+}^{\downarrow}
, which merge the vertical slices of the derivation.

The decomposition in
\mathsf{DDI}^{\downarrow}
follows the same pattern but takes only down-rules, so there is
no up-segment
.

Theorem 5.7

(Decomposition)
.

Let
A
and
B
be formulas.

1.

\mathsf{DDI}^{\downarrow}
-decomposition
: If
\mathcal{D}\mathbin{\,\triangleright\,}\mathsf{n}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{DDI}^{\downarrow}}}{{\hbox to12.58pt{\vbox to6.09pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.59999pt\lower-3.04544pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 15.75 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -3.94 L 0 3.94}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}A
, then (for some additive normal formulas
A^{\prime},A^{\prime\prime},A^{\prime\prime\prime}
) there is a derivation
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
in
\mathsf{DDI}^{\downarrow}
such that
\mathcal{D}\rightsquigarrow_{\mathsf{norm}}^{*}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
and

\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\mathbin{\,\triangleright\,}\mathsf{n}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\{{\mathsf{ai}}^{\downarrow}\}}}{{\hbox to12.58pt{\vbox to6.09pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.59999pt\lower-3.04544pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
