\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -3.94 L 0 3.94}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}{A^{\prime\prime\prime}}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\{\mathsf{s}\}}}{{\hbox to12.58pt{\vbox to6.09pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.59999pt\lower-3.04544pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 15.75 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}

Proof.

The decomposition of
\mathsf{DDI}^{\downarrow}
derivations follows from
Lemma

5.2
,
Lemma

5.4
.
2
and
Lemma

5.5
.
2
.

To prove decomposition of
\mathsf{SDDI}
derivations, we alternate applications of
Lemmas

5.2
and
5.4
until we obtain a derivation of the shape below.
Then we conclude by applying
Lemma

5.5
.
1
.

B\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\{{\mathsf{0}}^{\uparrow},{+}^{\uparrow}\}}}{{\hbox to12.58pt{\vbox to6.09pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.59999pt\lower-3.04544pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }

As a consequence, the up-fragment
\mathsf{DDI}^{\uparrow}
of
\mathsf{SDDI}
is
superfluous
(
Corollary

5.8
): all that can be proved in
\mathsf{SDDI}
, is already derivable in the down-fragment
\mathsf{DDI}^{\downarrow}
of
\mathsf{SDDI}
by a standard derivation.
The existence of a standard derivation in
\mathsf{DDI}^{\downarrow}
is obvious because the rule
{\mathsf{0}}^{\downarrow}
makes every
\mathsf{MELL}
formula derivable.
The interesting part is that a standard derivation in
\mathsf{DDI}^{\downarrow}
can be reached via normalization steps, hence in a computational way that is
internal
to
\mathsf{SDDI}
.
Indeed, normalization of
\mathsf{SDDI}
derivations follows from
\mathsf{SDDI}
decomposition (
Theorem

5.7
.
2
), so it relies on the normalization steps defined on
\mathsf{SDDI}
derivations (
Definition

5.6
).
This normalization result is the deep inference version of
cut-elimination
, since in
\mathsf{DDI}^{\downarrow}
there is no analogue of the rule
\mathsf{cut}
(
\mathsf{DDI}^{\downarrow}
is the “cut-free” fragment of
\mathsf{SDDI}
).

Corollary 5.8

(Normalization)
.

Let
A
be a formula and
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
If
\mathcal{D}\mathbin{\,\triangleright\,}\mathsf{n}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{SDDI}}}{{\hbox to12.58pt{\vbox to6.09pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.59999pt\lower-3.04544pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 15.75 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -3.94 L 0 3.94}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}A
then, for some
n^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a standard
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\mathbin{\,\triangleright\,}\mathsf{n}\!\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\{{\mathsf{0}}^{\uparrow},{+}^{\uparrow}\}}}{{\hbox to12.58pt{\vbox to6.09pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.59999pt\lower-3.04544pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -3.94 L 0 3.94}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}\mathsf{n}^{\prime}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{DDI}^{\downarrow}}}{{\hbox to12.58pt{\vbox to6.09pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.59999pt\lower-3.04544pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}A
such that

.
In particular,
\mathsf{n}^{\prime}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{DDI}^{\downarrow}}}{{\hbox to12.58pt{\vbox to6.09pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.59999pt\lower-3.04544pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 15.75 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -3.94 L 0 3.94}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}A
for some
n^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

By
Theorem

5.7
.
2
, if
\mathsf{n}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{SDDI}}}{{\hbox to12.58pt{\vbox to6.09pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.59999pt\lower-3.04544pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

then there is a standard derivation

\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}B\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\{{\mathsf{\wn d}}^{\uparrow},{\mathsf{\oc d}}^{\uparrow},{\mathsf{\oc w}}^{\uparrow},{\mathsf{\wn w}}^{\uparrow},{\mathsf{\wn c}}^{\uparrow},{\mathsf{\oc c}}^{\uparrow}\}}}{{\hbox to12.58pt{\vbox to6.09pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.59999pt\lower-3.04544pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

\mathsf{n}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\{{\mathsf{0}}^{\uparrow},{+}^{\uparrow}\}}}{{\hbox to12.58pt{\vbox to6.09pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.59999pt\lower-3.04544pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 15.75 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -3.94 L 0 3.94}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}B
implies that
B=\mathsf{n}^{\prime}
for some
n^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
.
As no rule in
\{{\mathsf{\wn w}}^{\uparrow},{\mathsf{\oc w}}^{\uparrow},{\mathsf{\wn c}}^{\uparrow},{\mathsf{\oc c}}^{\uparrow},{\mathsf{\wn d}}^{\uparrow},{\mathsf{\oc d}}^{\uparrow}\}
can be applied to a formula of the form
\mathsf{n}^{\prime}
, we have
\mathsf{n}^{\prime}=B^{\prime}
and we conclude by
Lemma

5.5
.
2
.
∎

6
Relation between Cut-elimination in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
and Normalization in
\mathsf{SDDI}

In this section we investigate the correspondence between the cut-elimination procedure
in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
sequent calculus (
Section

2
) and the normalization procedure in

\mathsf{SDDI}
(
Section

5
).

We provided a translation
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
of
\eta
-expanded
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
derivations to derivations in
\mathsf{DDI}^{\downarrow}\cup\{{\mathsf{i}}^{\uparrow}\}
(
Figure

6
) and so in
\mathsf{SDDI}
(via
Lemma

3.4
).
The translation preserves “cut-freeness” (
Theorem

4.1
), and exhibits a one-to-one correspondence between weakening, contraction and dereliction rules of the two systems.
However, the translation
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
does not commute with cut-elimination/normalization: an
\eta
-expanded derivation
\pi
in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
might reduce to a cut-free derivation
\widehat{\pi}
via cut-elimination, but its translation
\llbracket\pi\rrbracket
in
\mathsf{SDDI}
(including the transformation of the rules
{\mathsf{i}}^{\uparrow}
into
{\mathsf{ai}}^{\uparrow}
, as described in
Lemma

3.4
) normalizes to a
\mathsf{DDI}^{\downarrow}
derivation
\widehat{\llbracket\pi\rrbracket}
other than
\llbracket\widehat{\pi}\rrbracket
.
That is, diagram (
10
) does
not commute
:

\pi
\llbracket\pi\rrbracket
\llbracket\widehat{\pi}\rrbracket\neq\widehat{\llbracket\pi\rrbracket}
{\widehat{\pi}}
\llbracket~\rrbracket
\llbracket~\rrbracket
\mathsf{norm}
*
\mathsf{cut}
*

(10)

Technically, the lack of commutation is because
the rule
\mathsf{cut}
is translated as an instance of
{\mathsf{i}}^{\uparrow}
, which is not a rule of
\mathsf{SDDI}
(it is not an atomic cut) and hence has to be rewritten according to
Lemma

3.4
.
But this rewriting in
\mathsf{SDDI}
might not match the resource distribution of the corresponding
\mathsf{cut}
.
Consider the derivation
\pi
below in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
(with
\pi^{\prime}
cut-free and
\eta
-expanded) and its translation
\mathcal{D}_{\pi}
in
\mathsf{SDDI}
:

\footnotesize\pi\ =\ \box_set_eq_clear:cN{g_{_{e}}bproof_{b}ox_{1}}\char 15\relax\hbox{$\vbox{}$}\hskip 8.50012pt\underset{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket}{\overset{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde (
Lemma

3.4
) converts any potential interaction of weakening, contraction and dereliction up- and down-rules to an interaction of a (weakening, contraction or dereliction) down-rule with a dereliction up-rule: it arbitrarily chooses to asks for a resource, or to make it available, exactly once.
In our example, the translation creates the “mismatches”
{\mathsf{\wn w}}^{\downarrow}/{\mathsf{\oc d}}^{\uparrow}
and
{\mathsf{\oc w}}^{\downarrow}/{\mathsf{\wn d}}^{\uparrow}
even if in the original
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
derivation we had a “matched” interaction of a
\wn\mathsf{w}
with a
\oc\mathsf{w}
.
Due to these mismatches, the normal form of the derivation
\mathcal{D}_{\pi}
is

\mathsf{0}

\scriptstyle{\mathsf{0}}^{\downarrow}\;

\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket

,
which is not the translation of
\pi^{\prime}
(the normal form of
\pi
with respect to cut-elimination in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
) if
\pi^{\prime}\neq\box_set_eq_clear:cN{g_{_{e}}bproof_{b}ox_{1}}\char 15\relax\hbox{$\vbox{}$}
.
More generally, this problem is related to the fact that
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
misses the promotion rule
\mathsf{\oc p}
(
Figure

1
), which would make a resource available at will
[
14
]
(see
[
29
,
35
,
13
]
for its cut-elimination in
\mathsf{DiLL}=\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}\cup\{\mathsf{\oc p}\}
), while in the realm of
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
resources can be used only a finite number of times during cut-elimination.

In order to provide an
internal
solution (
i.e.
, in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
without adding the rule
\mathsf{\oc p}
to the system) to the commutation problem, we need to define a more sophisticated translation of
\mathsf{cut}
-rules into
\mathsf{SDDI}
.

A commutative translation.

We define a new translation
\llparenthesis\cdot\rrparenthesis
from
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
to
\mathsf{SDDI}
so that diagram (
10
) commutes, when
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
is replaced by
\llparenthesis\cdot\rrparenthesis
.
The idea is that the translation
\llparenthesis\cdot\rrparenthesis
“bends” a derivation
\pi
of

\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}}}{{\hbox to12.58pt{\vbox to6.09pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.59999pt\lower-3.04544pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}

to a derivation

\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\;\mathsf{SDDI}

\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket

so as to avoid using the rule
{\mathsf{i}}^{\uparrow}
.
In this way, roughly, the translation
\llparenthesis\cdot\rrparenthesis
converts the rule
\mathsf{cut}
below (where
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
are
\eta

\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
derivations) as follows:

.

To define properly the translation
\llparenthesis\pi\rrparenthesis
of an
\eta
-expanded
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
derivation, we first need to declare which formulas in
\pi
have to be “bent”, selecting one of the two cut formulas for each
\mathsf{cut}
in
\pi
.
Formally, given an
\eta
-expanded
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
derivation
\pi
, a
translation

\llparenthesis\pi\rrparenthesis
of
\pi
into
\mathsf{SDDI}
is defined in two steps.

1.

For each occurrence of the rule
\mathsf{cut}
in
\pi
, we mark exactly one of its two cut formulas, say
A
, with
{A}^{\bullet}
.
We propagate this mark bottom-up in
\pi
to the subformula occurrences of
A
in
\pi
: if the principal formula of a rule is marked, so are the active formulas in the premises (the other formulas in the sequent preserve their mark, if any).
For instance, if the conclusion of a rule
\invamp
is
\vdash{A}^{\bullet},{(B\invamp C)}^{\bullet},D
with
B\invamp C
as principal formula, then its premise is
\vdash{A}^{\bullet},{B}^{\bullet},{C}^{\bullet},D
.

2.

We translate
\pi
decorated with marks
{(\cdot)}^{\bullet}
into a derivation
\llparenthesis\pi\rrparenthesis
in
\mathsf{SDDI}
according to the definition in
Figure

9
(given by induction on
\pi
).

A)}^{\bullet},\Delta_{1},{\Delta}^{\bullet}_{2}$}}\kern 15.77057pt}}}\kern 5.48332pt}}}\overset{\llparenthesis\,\rrparenthesis}{\to}\ {\hbox{$\vbox{\vbox{\kern 0.4pt\hbox{\kern 0.4pt$\vbox{\hbox{\kern to8.0pt{\hbox{\vrule width=0.6pt,height=8.0pt\kern 1.2pt\vrule width=0.6pt,height=8.0pt}}$}}}$}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}\hss}\kern 10.92094pt}\kern-1.00462pt\hbox{\kern to0.0pt{\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}\hss}\kern 0.91121pt}\kern 1.00462pt\hbox{\kern 4.32779pt\hbox{\hbox{$\llbracket\Delta_{1}\rrbracket$}}\kern 0.91121pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}$}}}}$}\kern 0.0pt$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}$\kern 0.4pt}\kern 0.4pt}}$}}

Figure 9:
Translation of
\eta
-expanded
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
sequent calculus derivations into
\mathsf{SDDI}\setminus\{{\mathsf{ai}}^{\uparrow}\}
derivations (where if
\Gamma=A_{1},\dots,A_{n}
, then
{\Gamma}^{\bullet}={A_{1}}^{\bullet},\dots,{A_{n}}^{\bullet}
).

Note that an
\eta
-expanded derivation
\pi
in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
may have several translations
\llparenthesis\pi\rrparenthesis
, depending on the initial selection of cut formulas in
\pi
to mark.
We omit this dependency in the notation
\llparenthesis\pi\rrparenthesis
.
When we state a property of a translation
\llparenthesis\pi\rrparenthesis
, we mean that it holds for
any
initial selection of cut formulas in
\pi
.

Lemma 6.1

(Target of the translation
\llparenthesis\cdot\rrparenthesis
)
.

Let
\pi
be an
\eta
-expanded derivation in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
.
Then,
\llparenthesis\pi\rrparenthesis
is a derivation in
\mathsf{SDDI}
.
If, moreover,
\pi
is cut-free, then

\llparenthesis\pi\rrparenthesis=\mathcal{D}\circ\llbracket\pi\rrbracket
where
\mathcal{D}\mathbin{\,\triangleright\,}{\mathsf{1}}\!\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\{{\mathsf{0}}^{\uparrow},{+}^{\uparrow}\}}}{{\hbox to12.58pt{\vbox to6.09pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.59999pt\lower-3.04544pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 15.75 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -3.94 L 0 3.94}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}\mathsf{n}
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\llbracket\pi\rrbracket
(defined in
Figure

6
) is a derivation in
\mathsf{DDI}^{\downarrow}
.

Proof.

By induction on
\pi
.
Each step in
Figure

9
introduces only rules in
\mathsf{SDDI}
, in particular no step introduces the rule
{\mathsf{i}}^{\uparrow}
.
If
\pi
is cut-free, then no formula in
\pi
is marked;
thus, each step in
Figure

9
acts like
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
defined in
Figure

6
except possibly for adding some rules
{+}^{\uparrow}
and
{\mathsf{0}}^{\uparrow}
on top, and it does not introduce any other rule in
\mathsf{DDI}^{\uparrow}
except
\mathsf{s}
(which is also in
\mathsf{DDI}^{\downarrow}
).
∎

There is trivial way to “bend” a derivation
\pi
of
\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}}}{{\hbox to12.58pt{\vbox to6.09pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.59999pt\lower-3.04544pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 15.75 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -3.94 L 0 3.94}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}\Gamma,A
to a derivation in
\mathsf{SDDI}
: take

\bar{A}

\scriptstyle{\mathsf{0}}^{\uparrow}\;

\mathsf{0}

\scriptstyle{\mathsf{0}}^{\downarrow}\;

\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket

.
However, such a translation does not make diagram (
10
) commute, because it does not keep track of resources.
The translation
\llparenthesis\pi\rrparenthesis
, instead, is
resource-sensitive
, thanks to a one-to-one correspondence between the occurrences of rules for weakening, dereliction, and contraction in
\pi
and in
\llparenthesis\pi\rrparenthesis
(proved by induction on
\pi
).

Lemma 6.2

(Resources)
.

For any
\eta
-expanded derivation
\pi
in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
,
there is a one-to-one correspondence between the rule occurrences of
\oc\mathsf{d}
in
\pi
and the rule occurrences of
\{{\mathsf{\oc d}}^{\downarrow},{\mathsf{\oc d}}^{\uparrow}\}
in
\llparenthesis\pi\rrparenthesis
, and similarly between
\wn\mathsf{d}
and
\{{\mathsf{\wn d}}^{\downarrow},{\mathsf{\wn d}}^{\uparrow}\}
,
\oc\mathsf{c}
and
\{{\mathsf{\oc c}}^{\downarrow},{\mathsf{\oc c}}^{\uparrow}\}
,
\wn\mathsf{c}
and
\{{\mathsf{\wn c}}^{\downarrow},{\mathsf{\wn c}}^{\uparrow}\}
,
\oc\mathsf{w}
and
\{{\mathsf{\oc w}}^{\downarrow},{\mathsf{\oc w}}^{\uparrow}\}
,
\wn\mathsf{w}
and
\{{\mathsf{\wn w}}^{\downarrow},{\mathsf{\wn w}}^{\uparrow}\}
.

Let
\simeq_{\mathsf{norm}}
be the reflexive, transitive and symmetric closure of
\rightsquigarrow_{\mathsf{norm}}
.

Theorem 6.3

(Simulation)
.

If
\pi
is an
\eta
-expanded
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
derivation and
\pi\rightsquigarrow_{\mathsf{cut}}\pi^{\prime}
, then
\llparenthesis\pi\rrparenthesis\simeq_{\mathsf{norm}}\llparenthesis\pi^{\prime}\rrparenthesis
.

Proof (sketch).

By case analysis of cut-elimination steps for
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
, and the corresponding normalization steps in
\mathsf{SDDI}
.
By
Lemma

6.2
, cut-elimination steps for the rules in
\{\oc\mathsf{d},\wn\mathsf{d},\oc\mathsf{c},\wn\mathsf{c},\oc\mathsf{w},\wn\mathsf{w}\}
correspond to the (non-trivial) interaction-net rule permutations in
Figure

8
(
Lemma

5.4
.
1
).
A cut-elimination step
\otimes
/
\invamp
corresponds to linear permutations to prove
Lemma

5.5
.
Commutative cut-elimination steps correspond to trivial rule permutations.
All permutations are interleaved by steps (
6
) and (
8
),
in both directions.

∎

Theorem

6.3
says that normalization steps in
\mathsf{SDDI}
(
Definition

5.6
)
mimic
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
cut-elimination via translation
\llparenthesis\cdot\rrparenthesis
.
As a consequence, cut-elimination/normalization commutes with translation
\llparenthesis\cdot\rrparenthesis
.

Corollary 6.4

(Commutation)
.

If
\pi
is an
\eta
-expanded
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
derivation and
\pi\rightsquigarrow_{\mathsf{cut}}^{*}\hat{\pi}
with
\hat{\pi}
cut-free, then
\llparenthesis\pi\rrparenthesis\rightsquigarrow_{\mathsf{norm}}^{*}\llparenthesis\hat{\pi}\rrparenthesis
and
\llparenthesis\hat{\pi}\rrparenthesis
is normal for
\rightsquigarrow_{\mathsf{norm}}
.

Proof.

By simulation (
Theorem

6.3
), from
\pi\rightsquigarrow_{\mathsf{cut}}^{*}\hat{\pi}
it follows that
\llparenthesis\pi\rrparenthesis\simeq_{\mathsf{norm}}\llparenthesis\hat{\pi}\rrparenthesis
.
As
\hat{\pi}
is cut-free,
\llparenthesis\hat{\pi}\rrparenthesis
is normal for
\rightsquigarrow_{\mathsf{norm}}
according to
Lemma

6.1
, hence
\llparenthesis\pi\rrparenthesis\rightsquigarrow_{\mathsf{norm}}^{*}\llparenthesis\hat{\pi}\rrparenthesis
.
∎

7
Conclusions and Future Work

In this paper we introduced the first sound and complete deep inference system,
\mathsf{SDDI}
, for the promotion-free fragment of differential linear logic,
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}

[
10
]
.
The deep inference syntax recovers the symmetry of this logic lacking in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
sequent calculus—but which can be found in the interaction-net formalism for
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}

[
10
]
—and keeps the inductive and handy tree-like structure of sequent calculus derivations—missing in interaction nets.
The deep inference formalism allows us to reduce cuts to atomic formulas, and provides a tool for a more fine-grained study of cut-elimination.
Moreover, the syntax explicitly represents and internalizes the notion of slices of a derivation.

The inference rules of
\mathsf{SDDI}
present an up/down symmetry and we proved that the up-fragment of
\mathsf{SDDI}
is derivable from the down-fragment
\mathsf{DDI}^{\downarrow}
.
To prove this result we provided a normalization procedure based on rule permutations.
In fact, the presence of the connective
+
and its unit
\mathsf{0}
prevent the use of the general normalization result for splittable systems
[
38
]
.
In our normalization procedure for
\mathsf{SDDI}
, we are able to distinguish different kinds of rule permutations depending on their computational behavior:
some rule permutations correspond to linear (in terms of resource) cut-elimination steps, some to resource management cut-elimination steps and some to slice management operations.
Thanks to
Corollary

5.3
we could implement a reduction strategy alternating slice management and proper cut-elimination steps inside each slice.
The internal normalization procedure in
\mathsf{SDDI}
to prove
Corollary

5.8
provides “cut-free” derivations. And the translation
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
defined in
Figure

6
maps cut-free
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
derivations to
\mathsf{DDI}^{\downarrow}
, the “cut-free” fragment of
\mathsf{SDDI}
(
Theorem

4.3
.
2
).
We showed that cut-elimination/normalization does not commute with translation
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
, but it does with the translation
\llparenthesis\cdot\rrparenthesis
defined in
Figure

9
, a resource-sensitive refinement of
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
.

Translation of
\mathsf{DiLL}
proof-nets.

An ongoing work is to extend our deep inference system to represent the
full
differential linear logic
\mathsf{DiLL}=\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}\cup\{\mathsf{\oc p}\}

[
29
,
35
,
31
]
(including the promotion rule), possibly with the rule
\mathsf{mix}
which allows one to derive
A\invamp B
from
A\otimes B
.
The presence of promotion
\mathsf{\oc p}
allows us to define a translation that commutes with cut-elimination for the reasons discussed in
Section

6
.

In this extended deep inference system, we can translate not only the
\mathsf{DiLL}
sequent calculus but also
\mathsf{DiLL}
proof-nets, via a direct embedding that does not pass through the sequent calculus.
Indeed, the open deduction formalism
[
21
]
allows a direct encoding of proof-nets, plus a handy and inductive syntax.

Computational meaning and non-determinism.

In
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
interaction nets, when a cut-elimination step creates a new construct
\mathsf{sum}
(expressing a non-deterministic choice) or
\mathsf{zero}
(expressing mismatch on demanded and supplied resources), this construct is instantaneously propagated to all the interaction net where it is plugged in, without any computational step.
It is like
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
interaction nets allow one to deal with canonical forms only, in the sense of
Definition

2.1
.

A feature of our deep inference formalism is that the constructs
+
(non-determinism) and
\mathsf{0}
(resource mismatch) are internalized in the syntax, and when they appear during the normalization process, they are propagated all along the derivation by means of normalization steps (slice management,
Lemma

5.2
and
Corollary

5.3
).
Is there a computational meaning in these kind of steps? Is it possible to interpret them in a model of computation which intrinsically represents non-determinism, parallelism and concurrency?

The
\pi
-calculus
[
28
]
(a model of concurrent computation) can be encoded in
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}

[
7
]
, but Mazza
[
26
]
pointed out that the non-determinism expressed by
\mathsf{DiLL}_{0}
is too weak for true concurrency.
Deep inference may shed new light on the quest for a convincing proof-theoretic counterpart
of concurrency.

Acknowledgments.

The authors thank Andrea Aler Tubella, Alessio Guglielmi, Lutz Straßburger and the anonymous reviewers for their insightful comments.
This work has been partially supported by the EPSRC grant EP/R029121/1
“Typed Lambda-Calculi with Sharing and Unsharing”.
